[Lumen Field](206812.md), home of the [Seattle Seahawks](28390.md), [Seattle Sounders FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Sounders_FC), and [Seattle Reign FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Reign_FC)



[T-Mobile Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Mobile_Park), home of the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners)



[Climate Pledge Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Pledge_Arena), home of the [Seattle Kraken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Kraken), [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm), and [Seattle Torrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Torrent)



[Lumen Field](206812.md) during a Sounders match

Seattle has four major men's [professional sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_sports) teams: the [National Football League](21211.md) (NFL)'s [Seattle Seahawks](28390.md), [Major League Baseball](38776.md) (MLB)'s [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners), the [National Hockey League](21809.md) (NHL)'s [Seattle Kraken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Kraken), and [Major League Soccer](71802.md) (MLS)'s [Seattle Sounders FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Sounders_FC). Other professional sports teams include the [Seattle Seawolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Seawolves) of [Major League Rugby](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Rugby) (MLR). Seattle is the only U.S. city to have teams in three professional women's leagues: the [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) of the [Women's National Basketball Association](57721.md); [Seattle Reign FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Reign_FC) in the [National Women's Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League); and the [Seattle Torrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Torrent) in the [Professional Women's Hockey League](74699992.md).[^cite_note-255]

Seattle's professional sports history began at the start of the 20th century with the [PCHA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Coast_Hockey_Association)'s [Seattle Metropolitans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Metropolitans), which in 1917 became the first American hockey team to win the [Stanley Cup](66968.md).[^cite_note-256] In 1969, Seattle was awarded a Major League Baseball franchise, the [Seattle Pilots](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Pilots). Based at [Sick's Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sick's_Stadium) in [Mount Baker](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Baker,_Seattle), home to Seattle's [former minor-league teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Rainiers), the Pilots played in Seattle for [one season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1969_Major_League_Baseball_season) before relocating to [Milwaukee](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee) and becoming the [Milwaukee Brewers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Milwaukee_Brewers).[^cite_note-257] The city, alongside the county and state governments, sued the league and was offered a second expansion team, later named the [Seattle Mariners](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Mariners), as settlement.[^cite_note-258]

The Mariners began play in 1977 at the [multi-purpose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multi-purpose_stadium) [Kingdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdome), where the team struggled for most of its time. Relative success in the mid-to-late 1990s saved the team from being relocated and allowed them to move to a purpose-built baseball stadium, [T-Mobile Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/T-Mobile_Park) (formerly [Safeco Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Safeco_Field)), in 1999.[^cite_note-259][^cite_note-260] As of 2025, the Mariners are the only modern Major League Baseball franchise to have never reached a [World Series](23489161.md). They have only appeared in the MLB playoffs six times, the most recent in [2025](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Seattle_Mariners_season) (where they came within one win of advancing to the World Series), but have had Hall of Fame players and candidates like [Ken Griffey Jr.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ken_Griffey_Jr.), [Randy Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Randy_Johnson), [Ichiro Suzuki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ichiro_Suzuki), and [Alex Rodriguez](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alex_Rodriguez).[^cite_note-261] The team tied the all-time MLB single regular season wins record in 2001 with 116 wins.[^cite_note-262] From 2001 to 2022, the Mariners failed to qualify for the playoffs—the then-longest [active postseason drought](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Major_League_Baseball_franchise_postseason_droughts) in major North American sports, at 20 seasons.[^cite_note-263]

The Seattle Seahawks entered the National Football League in 1976 as an [expansion team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expansion_team) and have advanced to the [Super Bowl](27718.md) four times: [2005](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XL), [2013](4057023.md), [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XLIX), and [2025](63727016.md).[^cite_note-HL-Seahawks-264][^cite_note-Times-SBLX-265] The team played in the [Kingdome](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdome) until it was imploded in 2000 and moved into Qwest Field (now [Lumen Field](206812.md)) at the same site in 2002.[^cite_note-HL-Seahawks-264] The Seahawks lost [Super Bowl XL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XL) in 2005 to the [Pittsburgh Steelers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pittsburgh_Steelers) in [Detroit](8687.md), but won [Super Bowl XLVIII](4057023.md) in 2013 by defeating the [Denver Broncos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denver_Broncos) 43–8 at [MetLife Stadium](5476983.md). The team advanced to the Super Bowl the following year, but lost to the [New England Patriots](21719.md) in [Super Bowl XLIX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Super_Bowl_XLIX) on a last-minute play.[^cite_note-HL-Seahawks-264] In [Super Bowl LX](63727016.md), the Seahawks defeated the Patriots 29–13.[^cite_note-Times-SBLX-265] Seahawks fans have set stadium noise records on several occasions and are collectively known as the "[12th Man](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/12th_man_\(football\))".[^cite_note-HL-Seahawks-264][^cite_note-266]

Seattle Sounders FC has played in Major League Soccer since 2009, as the latest continuation of the [original 1974 Sounders team](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Sounders_\(1974%E2%80%931983\)) of the [North American Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Soccer_League_\(1968%E2%80%931984\)) after [an incarnation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Sounders_\(1994%E2%80%932008\)) in the [lower divisions of American soccer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_soccer_pyramid).[^cite_note-267] Sharing Lumen Field with the Seahawks, the team set various [attendance records](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Soccer_attendance) in its first few MLS seasons, averaging over 43,000 per match and placing themselves among the top 30 teams internationally.[^cite_note-268][^cite_note-269] The Sounders have won the [MLS Supporters' Shield](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Supporters'_Shield) in 2014[^cite_note-270] and the [U.S. Open Cup](253156.md) on four occasions: [2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2009_U.S._Open_Cup), [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_U.S._Open_Cup), [2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011_U.S._Open_Cup), and [2014](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2014_U.S._Open_Cup).[^cite_note-271] The Sounders won the first of their two [MLS Cup](483246.md) titles in [2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Cup_2016), defeating [Toronto FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Toronto_FC) 5–4 in a [penalty shootout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Penalty_shootout_\(association_football\)) in Toronto,[^cite_note-272] before finishing as runners-up in a rematch against Toronto in [MLS Cup 2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Cup_2017). In [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Cup_2019) the Sounders made their first-ever home-field appearance in MLS Cup, once again against Toronto FC, and won the game 3–1 to earn their second MLS Cup title in front of a club-record attendance of 69,274.[^cite_note-273] The stadium also hosted the second leg of the [2022 CONCACAF Champions League Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_CONCACAF_Champions_League_Final), played in front of 68,741 to break the tournament attendance record. The Sounders became the first MLS team to win a continental title since 2000 and the first to win the modern [Champions League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CONCACAF_Champions_League).[^cite_note-274]

Seattle's Major League Rugby team, the [Seattle Seawolves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Seawolves), play in nearby [Tukwila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tukwila,_Washington) at the [Starfire Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Starfire_Sports_Complex), a small stadium that is also used by the Sounders for their [U.S. Open Cup](253156.md) matches.[^cite_note-275] The team began play in 2018 and won [the league's inaugural championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_Major_League_Rugby_final).[^cite_note-276] They successfully defended their title in the [2019 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Major_League_Rugby_final) and finished as runners-up in the [2022 championship game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Major_League_Rugby_final).[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

From 1967 to 2008, Seattle was home to the [Seattle SuperSonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_SuperSonics) of the [National Basketball Association](22093.md) (NBA), the city's first major professional sports team. A frequent playoff participant, the Sonics were the [1978–79 NBA champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_NBA_Finals), and also contended for the championship in [1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_NBA_Finals) and [1996](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1996_NBA_Finals). Following a team sale in 2006, a failed effort to replace the aging [KeyArena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KeyArena), and settlement of a lawsuit to hold the team to the final two years of its lease with the city, [the SuperSonics relocated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_SuperSonics_relocation_to_Oklahoma_City) to [Oklahoma City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oklahoma_City) and became the [Oklahoma City Thunder](18256220.md) ahead of the [2008–09 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008%E2%80%9309_NBA_season).[^cite_note-279][^cite_note-280] An [effort in 2013](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Failed_relocation_of_the_Sacramento_Kings#Seattle) to purchase the [Sacramento Kings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sacramento_Kings) franchise and relocate it to Seattle as a resurrected Sonics squad was denied by the NBA board of governors.[^cite_note-281]

The [Seattle Storm](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Storm) of the [Women's National Basketball Association](57721.md) have also played their games at KeyArena (now [Climate Pledge Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Pledge_Arena)) since their foundation in 2000. The WNBA granted Seattle their expansion side following the popularity of the recently folded [Seattle Reign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Reign_\(basketball\)), a women's professional basketball team that played from 1996 to 1998 in the rival [American Basketball League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Basketball_League_\(1996%E2%80%931998\)).[^cite_note-seattletimes_121912-282] The Storm began as a sister team to the now-defunct [Sonics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_SuperSonics) of the NBA, but sold to separate Seattle-based ownership in 2006. Tied for the league record, the Storm have claimed the [WNBA championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Finals) on four occasions, winning in [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_WNBA_Finals), [2010](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_WNBA_Finals), [2018](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2018_WNBA_Finals), and [2020](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2020_WNBA_Finals).[^cite_note-283][^cite_note-284] The team also won the first-ever [WNBA Commissioner's Cup](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup) in [2021](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2021_WNBA_Commissioner's_Cup).

The [Seattle Thunderbirds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Thunderbirds) hockey team has represented Seattle in the Canadian major-junior [Western Hockey League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Hockey_League) since 1977. Originally playing in [Mercer Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercer_Arena) and the [Seattle Center Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Center_Coliseum) (which had hosted [previous minor-league hockey teams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ice_hockey_in_Seattle)), the Thunderbirds have been based at the [ShoWare Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ShoWare_Center) in the suburb of [Kent](138005.md) since 2007, and have won one WHL championship in [2017](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016%E2%80%9317_WHL_season).[^cite_note-285] In 1974, Seattle was [awarded a conditional expansion franchise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Totems) in the [National Hockey League](21809.md); however, this opportunity did not come to fruition. In 2018, a new Seattle-based group successfully applied for an expansion team in the NHL, which was named the [Seattle Kraken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Kraken) and began play in 2021.[^cite_note-286][^cite_note-SeattleApplies-287][^cite_note-288] The SuperSonics' former home arena, KeyArena (now [Climate Pledge Arena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_Pledge_Arena)), underwent major renovations from 2018 to 2021 to accommodate the new NHL team.[^cite_note-289] The NHL ownership group reached its goal of 10,000 deposits within 12 minutes of opening a ticket drive, which later increased to 25,000 in 75 minutes.[^cite_note-290]

[Seattle Reign FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Reign_FC),[^cite_note-Reign-291] a founding member of the [National Women's Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Women's_Soccer_League), was founded in 2012, holding their home games in Seattle from 2014 to 2018 and again since 2022. The team name was chosen to honor the [defunct women's basketball team of the same name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Reign_\(basketball\)).[^cite_note-seattletimes_121912-282] The club played at the Starfire Sports Complex in nearby Tukwila for the league's inaugural 2013 season before moving to [Seattle Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Center)'s [Memorial Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Memorial_Stadium_\(Seattle\)) in 2014. Under new management, the team moved to [Tacoma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tacoma,_Washington)'s [Cheney Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cheney_Stadium) in 2019, before moving to Seattle's Lumen Field in 2022.[^cite_note-OLR-Return-292] In 2020, OL Groupe, the [parent company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parent_company) of French clubs [Olympique Lyonnais](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympique_Lyonnais) and [Olympique Lyonnais Féminin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Olympique_Lyonnais_F%C3%A9minin), became the team's majority owner and rebranded the club as OL Reign.[^cite_note-OLR-Return-292] The Seattle Reign name was restored in 2024.[^cite_note-293]

The [Seattle Torrent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Torrent) are one of the first two expansion teams, along with the [Vancouver Goldeneyes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vancouver_Goldeneyes), in the [Professional Women's Hockey League](74699992.md) (PWHL), the top-tier women's [ice hockey](14790.md) league in the United States and Canada. The team were founded in 2025 and began play in the [2025–26 season](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025%E2%80%9326_PWHL_season).[^cite_note-Times-PWHL-294] Supported by the NHL's Seattle Kraken, the team plays its home games at Climate Pledge Arena.[^cite_note-295]

Seattle has also been home to various minor-league professional teams, of which currently [Ballard FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballard_FC) and [West Seattle Junction FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Seattle_Junction_FC) of [USL League 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_League_2) in soccer remain. Representing the Seattle neighborhood of [Ballard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballard,_Seattle), Ballard FC was founded in 2022 as an independent, semi-professional soccer team in the fourth-division [USL League 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USL_League_2). The team is owned by a group led by former Sounders player [Lamar Neagle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lamar_Neagle) and won [its first national title in 2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_USL_League_Two_season). Ballard FC's primary home is the 1,000-seat Interbay Soccer Stadium (also home to [Seattle Pacific University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Pacific_University)'s and [Ballard High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballard_High_School_\(Seattle\))'s soccer teams), but during that field's renovations in the 2024 season, Ballard will play out of Memorial Stadium at the Seattle Center.[^cite_note-296][^cite_note-297] [West Seattle Junction FC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Seattle_Junction_FC), representing the neighborhood of [West Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Seattle), joined USL League 2 during the 2024 season.[^cite_note-USL-WestSeattle-254]

The short-lived [Seattle Sea Dragons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Sea_Dragons), originally the Dragons, of the [XFL](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/XFL_\(2020\)) played at Lumen Field in the league's inaugural season in 2020 prior to its suspension due to the COVID-19 pandemic.[^cite_note-298] The Sea Dragons returned alongside the XFL in [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_XFL_season) after the league's re-launch under new ownership.[^cite_note-299] The team folded prior to the 2024 season during the XFL's merger with the [United States Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Football_League_\(2022\)) to form the [United Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Football_League_\(2024\)).[^cite_note-300]

Seattle also boasts two collegiate sports programs based at the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) and [Seattle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_University), both competing in [NCAA Division I](669931.md) for various sports.[^cite_note-NCAA-301] The University of Washington's athletic program, nicknamed the [Huskies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Huskies), competes in the [Big Ten Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Ten_Conference), and Seattle University's athletic program, nicknamed the [Redhawks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Redhawks), mostly competes in the [Western Athletic Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Athletic_Conference). The Huskies teams use several facilities, including the 70,000-seat [Husky Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Husky_Stadium) for [football](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Huskies_football) and the [Hec Edmundson Pavilion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hec_Edmundson_Pavilion) for basketball and volleyball.[^cite_note-302][^cite_note-303] The two schools have basketball and soccer teams that compete against each other in non-conference games and have formed a local rivalry due to their sporting success.[^cite_note-NCAA-301]

The [Major League Baseball All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game) has been held in Seattle three times, once at the Kingdome in [1979](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game), and twice at T-Mobile Park in [2001](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2001_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game) and [2023](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2023_Major_League_Baseball_All-Star_Game).[^cite_note-304] The [NBA All-Star Game](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/NBA_All-Star_Game) was also held in Seattle twice: the first in [1974](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1974_NBA_All-Star_Game) at the [Seattle Center Coliseum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Center_Coliseum) and the second in [1987](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1987_NBA_All-Star_Game) at the Kingdome.[^cite_note-305] [Lumen Field](206812.md) hosted [MLS Cup 2009](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/MLS_Cup_2009), played between [Real Salt Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Salt_Lake) and the [Los Angeles Galaxy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Los_Angeles_Galaxy), as a neutral site in front of 46,011 spectators.[^cite_note-306] Seattle was one of eleven US host cities for the [2026 FIFA World Cup](36581929.md), with matches played at Lumen Field and training facilities at [Longacres](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Longacres) in [Renton, Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renton,_Washington).[^cite_note-307]

## Parks and recreation

See also: [List of parks in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parks_in_Seattle) and [Seattle Parks and Recreation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Parks_and_Recreation)



[Lake Union Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Union_Park) at the southern end of [Lake Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lake_Union)

Seattle's mild, temperate marine climate allows year-round outdoor recreation, including walking, cycling, hiking, skiing, snowboarding, kayaking, rock climbing, motorboating, sailing, team sports, and swimming.[^cite_note-308] The [city parks system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Parks_and_Recreation) encompasses 485 parks, shorelines, and preserved spaces that total 6,500 acres (2,600 ha)—12 percent of the land area of Seattle. These city-owned facilities include 25 miles (40 km) of boulevards and 120 miles (190 km) of walking and hiking trails, athletic fields, swimming pools, community centers, bathhouses, and performance spaces.[^cite_note-309] The [Trust for Public Land](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trust_for_Public_Land) ranked Seattle eighth in the United States among municipal parks systems in 2023 and estimates that 99 percent of residents live within a 1⁄2-mile (0.8 km) of a park.[^cite_note-310]

The largest park in the city is [Discovery Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discovery_Park_\(Seattle\)), which includes 534 acres (2.16 km<sup>2</sup>) of forestland and saltwater beaches along the bluffs in [Magnolia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magnolia,_Seattle).[^cite_note-311] Among the most popular Seattle parks are [Green Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Green_Lake_\(Seattle\)), which is ringed by a walking trail; [Alki Beach Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alki_Beach_Park) on the southwest side of Elliott Bay; [Myrtle Edwards Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Myrtle_Edwards_Park) near the downtown waterfront; [Volunteer Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volunteer_Park_\(Seattle\)) on Capitol Hill; and [Seward Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seward_Park_\(Seattle\)) on Lake Washington.[^cite_note-312] Several city parks include panoramic views of the Seattle skyline, including [Kerry Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kerry_Park) on Queen Anne Hill and [Gas Works Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gas_Works_Park) in [Wallingford](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallingford,_Seattle), which features the preserved [superstructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Superstructure) of a [coal gasification](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coal_gasification) plant closed in 1956.[^cite_note-313]

Seattle has a network of recreational and commuting trails for cyclists and pedestrians, mainly repurposed from disused railroads or built alongside regional highways. The [Burke–Gilman Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Burke%E2%80%93Gilman_Trail), which travels for 27 miles (43 km) along the Ship Canal and Lake Washington between Ballard and [Bothell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bothell,_Washington), first opened in 1978 on a former railroad.[^cite_note-314] The [Mountains to Sound Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountains_to_Sound_Greenway) connects the Interstate 90 corridor, including the north side of the [Homer M. Hadley Memorial Bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homer_M._Hadley_Memorial_Bridge), and is planned to be extended to Snoqualmie.[^cite_note-315] Other non-motorized paths include the [Overlook Walk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overlook_Walk), which opened in 2024 to connect Pike Place Market to the downtown waterfront via a set of overpasses that integrate with an expansion of the Seattle Aquarium.[^cite_note-316] Also popular among Seattle residents are hikes and skiing in the nearby Cascade or Olympic Mountains and kayaking and sailing in the region's waterways.[^cite_note-317]

## Government and politics

Main articles: [Government and politics of Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_and_politics_of_Seattle), [Mayor of Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Seattle), and [Seattle City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_City_Council)



[Seattle City Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_City_Council) consists of two at-large positions and seven district seats representing the areas shown from 2016 to 2023.

Seattle is a [charter city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charter_city), with a [mayor–council form of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor%E2%80%93council_government). From 1911 to 2013, Seattle's nine city councillors were elected at large, rather than by geographic subdivisions.[^cite_note-archive-chrono-318] For the 2015 election, this changed to a hybrid system of seven district members and two at-large members as a result of a ballot measure passed on November 5, 2013. The only other elected offices are the [city attorney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_attorney) and Municipal Court judges. All city offices are officially [non-partisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-partisan_democracy).[^cite_note-319] Like some other parts of the United States, government and laws are also run by a series of ballot initiatives (allowing citizens to pass or reject laws), referendums (allowing citizens to approve or reject legislation already passed), and propositions (allowing specific government agencies to propose new laws or tax increases directly to the people).[^cite_note-320]

Seattle is widely considered one of the most socially liberal cities in the United States.[^cite_note-321] In the 2012 U.S. general election, a majority of Seattleites voted to approve Referendum 74 and legalize gay marriage in Washington state.[^cite_note-322] In the same election, an overwhelming majority of Seattleites also voted to approve the legalization of the recreational use of [cannabis](1481886.md) in the state.[^cite_note-323] Like much of the [Pacific Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Northwest) (which has the lowest rate of [church attendance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_attendance) in the United States and consistently reports the highest percentage of [atheism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheism)[^cite_note-324][^cite_note-325]), church attendance, religious belief, and political influence of religious leaders are much lower than in other parts of America.[^cite_note-326] Seattle's political culture is very liberal and [progressive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Progressivism_in_the_United_States) for the United States, with over 80% of the population voting for the [Democratic Party](5043544.md). All precincts in Seattle voted for Democratic Party candidate [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) in the [2012 presidential election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election).[^cite_note-Liberal-327] In partisan elections for the [Washington State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Legislature) and [United States Congress](31756.md), nearly all elections are won by Democrats. Although local elections are nonpartisan, most of the city's elected officials are known to be Democrats.

In 1926, Seattle became the first major American city to elect a female mayor, [Bertha Knight Landes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertha_Knight_Landes).[^cite_note-328] It has also elected an openly gay mayor, [Ed Murray](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Murray_\(Washington_politician\)),[^cite_note-329] and a third-party socialist councillor, [Kshama Sawant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kshama_Sawant).[^cite_note-330] For the first time in United States history, an openly gay black woman was elected to public office when Sherry Harris was elected as a Seattle city councilor in 1991.[^cite_note-331][^cite_note-332] In 2015, the majority of the city council was female.[^cite_note-333]

[Katie Wilson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katie_Wilson) was elected as mayor in the [2025 mayoral election](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2025_Seattle_mayoral_election), by a margin of 2,000 votes, and defeated incumbent [Bruce Harrell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruce_Harrell). She took office on January 1, 2026, becoming the third woman to serve as mayor.[^cite_note-334] The mayor's office also includes three [deputy mayors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deputy_mayor), appointed to advise the mayor on policies.

In 2023, the city council voted to ban [caste discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caste_discrimination_in_the_United_States) as part of the city's anti-discrimination laws. The ban is the first in the United States.[^cite_note-335]

Seattle lies within four districts on the [King County Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County_Council): the 1st district includes the northeastern corner of the city; the 2nd district generally covers areas east of [Interstate 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_5_in_Washington) and south of Northeast 65th Street; the 4th district consists of the northwestern neighborhoods of Ballard, Fremont, Magnolia, and Queen Anne; and the 8th district includes Downtown Seattle, First Hill, SODO, and West Seattle.[^cite_note-336] At the state level, Seattle is divided into six districts that each have one [state senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Senate) and two [state representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_House_of_Representatives).[^cite_note-337][^cite_note-338]

Federally, Seattle is split between two congressional districts. Most of the city is in [7th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington's_7th_congressional_district),[^cite_note-King-Congressional-339] represented by Democrat [Pramila Jayapal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pramila_Jayapal), the first Indian-American woman elected to Congress. She succeeded 28-year incumbent and fellow Democrat [Jim McDermott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jim_McDermott).[^cite_note-340] Part of southeastern Seattle is in the [9th congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington's_9th_congressional_district),[^cite_note-King-Congressional-339] represented by Democrat [Adam Smith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adam_Smith_\(Washington_politician\)) since 1997.[^cite_note-341] The border between the two districts follows the Tukwila city limits around Boeing Field, Interstate 5, South Dearborn Street, 4th Avenue South, James Street, Madison Street, East Union Street, Martin Luther King Jr. Way, and East Yesler Way.[^cite_note-King-Congressional-339]

## Education

Main article: [Education in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Seattle)

Further information: [List of libraries in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_libraries_in_Seattle) and [Seattle Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Public_Schools)

|  | This section needs to be **updated**. Please help update this article to reflect recent events or newly available information. *(April 2021)* |
| --- | --- |

Of the city's population over the age of 25, 53.8% (vs. a national average of 27.4%) hold a [bachelor's degree](188874.md) or higher, and 91.9% (vs. 84.5% nationally) have a high school diploma or [equivalent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Educational_Development). A 2008 United States Census Bureau survey showed that Seattle had the highest percentage of college and university graduates of any major U.S. city.[^cite_note-Most_Educated_City_in_US-342] The city was listed as the most literate of the country's 69 largest cities in 2005 and 2006, the second most literate in 2007 and the most literate in 2008 in studies conducted by [Central Connecticut State University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Connecticut_State_University).[^cite_note-343]

[Seattle Public Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Public_Schools) is the school district for the vast majority of the city.[^cite_note-KingCoSDMap-344] The school district desegregated without a court order[^cite_note-345] but still struggles to achieve racial balance in a somewhat ethnically divided city (the south part of town having more ethnic minorities than the north).[^cite_note-346] In 2007, Seattle's racial tie-breaking system was struck down by the [United States Supreme Court](31737.md), but the ruling left the door open for desegregation formulae based on other indicators (e.g., income or socioeconomic class).[^cite_note-347] A small portion of the city's [Delridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Delridge,_Seattle) neighborhood lies within the boundaries of the [Highline School District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Highline_School_District).[^cite_note-KingCoSDMap-344]

The public school system is supplemented by a moderate number of private schools: Five of the private high schools are [Catholic](606848.md), one is [Lutheran](23371382.md), and six are [secular](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secular).[^cite_note-348]

University of Washington Quad

[University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) Quad

Seattle is home to the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) and its professional and continuing education unit, the [University of Washington Continuum College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Continuum_College). In 2017, *[U.S. News & World Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._News_&_World_Report)* ranked the University of Washington eleventh in the world.[^cite_note-349] The UW receives more federal research and development funding than any public institution. Over the last 10 years, it has also produced more [Peace Corps](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peace_Corps) volunteers than any other U.S. university.[^cite_note-350]

Seattle also has a number of smaller private universities, including [Seattle University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_University) and [Seattle Pacific University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Pacific_University), the former a Jesuit Catholic institution, the latter a Free Methodist institution. The [Seattle Colleges District](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Colleges_District) operates three colleges: [North Seattle College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Seattle_College), [Seattle Central College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Central_College), and [South Seattle College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Seattle_College). Universities aimed at the working adult are the [City University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_University_of_Seattle) and [Antioch University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antioch_University). Seminaries include [Western Seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_Seminary) and a number of arts colleges, such as [Cornish College of the Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cornish_College_of_the_Arts), [Pratt Fine Arts Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pratt_Fine_Arts_Center). In 2001, *Time* magazine selected Seattle Central Community College as community college of the year, saying that the school "pushes diverse students to work together in small teams."[^cite_note-351]

## Media

Main article: [Media in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Media_in_Seattle)



[KEXP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KEXP)'s Seattle Center Studio. Public concerts are occasionally hosted inside.

*[The Seattle Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Seattle_Times)* is the lone daily newspaper in the city and was founded in 1896; it is one of the few remaining family-owned major newspapers in the United States.[^cite_note-352] The *[Seattle Post-Intelligencer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Post-Intelligencer)*, known as the *P-I*, published a daily newspaper from 1863 to March 17, 2009, before switching to a strictly online publication.[^cite_note-353] Other daily newspapers in the city include the business publication *[Seattle Daily Journal of Commerce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Daily_Journal_of_Commerce)* and the University of Washington's student-run newspaper *[The Daily](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Daily_of_the_University_of_Washington)*.[^cite_note-AP-Papers-354] The most prominent weeklies are the *[Seattle Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Weekly)* and *[The Stranger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Stranger_\(newspaper\))*; both consider themselves ["alternative" papers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alternative_newspaper).[^cite_note-355] The weekly LGBT newspaper is the *[Seattle Gay News](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Gay_News)*. *[Real Change](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Real_Change)* is a weekly [street newspaper](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_newspaper) that is sold mainly by [homeless](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Homeless) persons as an alternative to [panhandling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Begging). There are also several ethnic newspapers, including *[The Facts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Facts_\(Seattle\))*, *[Northwest Asian Weekly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwest_Asian_Weekly)* and the *[International Examiner](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Examiner)*. Several Seattle neighborhoods had weekly publications, including the *[Ballard News-Tribune](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ballard_News-Tribune)*, *[Capitol Hill Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Capitol_Hill_Times)*, and *[West Seattle Herald](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Seattle_Herald)*, that were shut down in the early 21st century; hyperlocal neighborhood blogs have emerged as an alternative to these weeklies.[^cite_note-356]

Seattle is also well served by television and radio, with all major U.S. networks represented, along with at least five other English-language stations and two Spanish-language stations.[^cite_note-357] Seattle cable viewers also receive [CBUT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBUT) 2 ([CBC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/CBC_Television)) from [Vancouver](32706.md), British Columbia.

[Non-commercial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-commercial) radio stations include [NPR](29697232.md) member stations [KUOW-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KUOW-FM) 94.9 and [KNKX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KNKX) 88.5 (Tacoma), as well as [classical music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music) station [KING-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KING-FM) 98.1. Other non-commercial stations include [KEXP-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KEXP-FM) 90.3 (affiliated with the UW), [community radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_radio) [KBCS-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KBCS-FM) 91.3 (affiliated with [Bellevue College](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellevue_College)), and [high school radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High_school_radio) [KNHC-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KNHC-FM) 89.5, which broadcasts an [electronic dance music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electronic_dance_music) [radio format](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Radio_format), is owned by the public school system and operated by students of [Nathan Hale High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nathan_Hale_High_School_\(Washington\)). Many Seattle radio stations are available through [Internet radio](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_radio), with KEXP in particular being a pioneer of Internet radio.[^cite_note-358] Seattle also has numerous commercial radio stations. In a March 2012 report by the consumer research firm [Arbitron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arbitron), the top FM stations were [KRWM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KRWM) ([adult contemporary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Adult_contemporary) format), [KIRO-FM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KIRO-FM) ([news/talk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/News/talk)), and [KISW](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KISW) ([active rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Active_rock)) while the top AM stations were [KNWN (Formerly KOMO)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KNWN_\(AM\)) ([all news](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_news)), [KJR (AM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KJR_\(AM\)) ([all sports](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sports_radio)), [KIRO (AM)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/KIRO_\(AM\)) (all sports).[^cite_note-359]

## Infrastructure

### Health systems

Main article: [Medical facilities of Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medical_facilities_of_Seattle)



[Seattle Children's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Children's) in [Laurelhurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurelhurst,_Seattle)

The University of Washington is consistently ranked among the country's leading institutions in medical research, earning special merits for programs in neurology and neurosurgery. The university-run [UW Medicine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UW_Medicine) system encompasses several major local hospitals, including [Harborview Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harborview_Medical_Center), the public county hospital and the only Level I [trauma hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trauma_center) for Washington, Alaska, Montana, and Idaho.[^cite_note-360] Harborview and two other major hospitals—[Virginia Mason Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Virginia_Mason_Medical_Center) and [Swedish Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swedish_Medical_Center)—are located on First Hill, which is nicknamed "Pill Hill" for its concentration of medical facilities.[^cite_note-361]

Located in the [Laurelhurst](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laurelhurst,_Seattle) neighborhood, [Seattle Children's](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Children's), formerly [Children's Hospital and Regional Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Children's_Hospital_and_Regional_Medical_Center), is the pediatric referral center for Washington, Alaska, Montana, and Idaho. The [Fred Hutchinson Cancer Research Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fred_Hutchinson_Cancer_Research_Center) has a campus in the Eastlake neighborhood. The University District is home to the [University of Washington Medical Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington_Medical_Center) which, along with Harborview, is operated by the University of Washington. Seattle is also served by a [Veterans Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Veterans_Affairs) hospital on [Beacon Hill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beacon_Hill,_Seattle), a third campus of Swedish in Ballard, and [UW Medical Center - Northwest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UW_Medical_Center_-_Northwest) near [Northgate Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northgate_Station_\(shopping_mall\)).[^cite_note-362]

Seattle has seen local developments of modern paramedic services with the establishment of [Medic One](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medic_One) in 1970.[^cite_note-cobb-363] In 1974, a *[60 Minutes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/60_Minutes)* story on the success of the then four-year-old Medic One paramedic system called Seattle "the best place in the world to have a heart attack."[^cite_note-364] Seattle has several [pharmacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pharmacy) chains; these include [Walgreens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walgreens), and formerly [Bartell Drugs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bartell_Drugs), which was family-run in Seattle until its acquisition by [Rite Aid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rite_Aid) in 2020 and then closed in 2025.[^cite_note-365][^cite_note-366] As of 2024, Seattle lacks a 24-hour retail pharmacy due to the closure of locations across several chains.[^cite_note-367][^cite_note-368]

### Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Seattle)

Further information: [Street layout of Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_layout_of_Seattle)

See also: [List of bridges in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_bridges_in_Seattle)



[Interstate 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_5_in_Washington) passing through downtown Seattle



[King County Water Taxi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County_Water_Taxi) and downtown Seattle



[1 Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Line_\(Sound_Transit\)) light rail trains in the [Downtown Seattle Transit Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Downtown_Seattle_Transit_Tunnel) at [University Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_Street_\(Link_station\))



[King Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Street_Station), the 15th-busiest [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) station in the nation, also serves commuter trains.

The [first streetcars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Street_Railway) appeared in 1889 and were instrumental in the creation of a relatively well-defined downtown and strong neighborhoods at the end of their lines. The advent of the automobile began the dismantling of rail in Seattle. Tacoma–Seattle railway service ended in 1929 and the Everett–Seattle service came to an end in 1939, replaced by automobiles running on the recently developed highway system. Rails on city streets were paved over or removed, and the opening of the [Seattle trolleybus system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trolleybuses_in_Seattle) brought the end of [streetcars in Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Street_Railway) in 1941. This left an extensive network of privately owned buses (later public) as the only mass transit within the city and throughout the region.[^cite_note-interurbanrail-369]

[King County Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_County_Metro) provides regular bus service in the city and county, and the [South Lake Union Streetcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Lake_Union_Streetcar) line and the [First Hill Streetcar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Hill_Streetcar) line.[^cite_note-slustreetcar-370] Seattle is one of the few cities in North America whose bus fleet includes electric trolleybuses. [Sound Transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_Transit) provides an express bus service within the metropolitan area, two [Sounder commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sounder_commuter_rail) lines between the suburbs and downtown, and its [1 Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Line_\(Sound_Transit\)) [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) line between [Northgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northgate,_Seattle) and Angle Lake.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372]

[Washington State Ferries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Ferries), which manages the largest network of ferries in the United States and third-largest in the world, connects Seattle to [Bainbridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bainbridge_Island,_Washington) and [Vashon Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vashon,_Washington) in Puget Sound and to Bremerton and [Southworth](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southworth,_Washington) on the Kitsap Peninsula.[^cite_note-ferries-373] In addition to the state's large car ferries, passenger-only [catamarans](195952.md) are operated by other public agencies to provide service on water taxi and "fast ferry" routes.[^cite_note-374] [King Street Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Street_Station) in Pioneer Square serves [Amtrak](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amtrak) intercity trains and Sounder commuter trains, and is located adjacent to the [International District/Chinatown light rail station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_District/Chinatown_station).[^cite_note-375]

According to the 2007 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey), 18.6% of Seattle residents used one of the three public transit systems that serve the city, giving it the highest transit ridership of all major cities without heavy or light rail prior to the completion of Sound Transit's 1 Line.[^cite_note-376] The city has also been described by [Bert Sperling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bert_Sperling) as the fourth most walkable U.S. city and by [Walk Score](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walk_Score) as the sixth most walkable of the fifty largest U.S. cities.[^cite_note-377][^cite_note-378]

[Seattle–Tacoma International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle%E2%80%93Tacoma_International_Airport), locally known as Sea-Tac Airport and located just south in the neighboring city of SeaTac, is operated by the Port of Seattle and provides commercial air service to destinations throughout the world. Closer to downtown, [Boeing Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_Field) is used for general aviation, cargo flights, and testing/delivery of Boeing airliners. A secondary passenger airport, [Paine Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paine_Field), opened in 2019 and is located in [Everett](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Everett,_Washington), 25 miles (40 km) north of Seattle. It is predominantly used by Boeing and their [large assembly plant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boeing_Everett_Factory) located nearby.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

The main mode of transportation is the street system, which is laid out in a [cardinal directions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cardinal_direction) [grid pattern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grid_plan), except in the central business district where early city leaders [Arthur Denny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arthur_A._Denny) and [Carson Boren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Carson_Boren) insisted on orienting the plats relative to the shoreline rather than to true north.[^cite_note-381] The city's topography, formed by the recession of glaciers, created north–south troughs that did not allow east–west streets to be continuous;[^cite_note-382] only [Madison Street](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madison_Street_\(Seattle\)) runs uninterrupted from Elliott Bay to Lake Washington.[^cite_note-383] Only two roads, [Interstate 5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_5_in_Washington) and [State Route 99](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_State_Route_99) (both limited-access highways) run uninterrupted through the city from north to south.[^cite_note-384]

From 1953 to 2019, State Route 99 ran through downtown Seattle on the [Alaskan Way Viaduct](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaskan_Way_Viaduct), an elevated freeway on the waterfront. Due to damage sustained during the 2001 Nisqually earthquake the viaduct was replaced by a tunnel. The 2-mile (3.2 km) [Alaskan Way Viaduct replacement tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alaskan_Way_Viaduct_replacement_tunnel) was originally scheduled to be completed in December 2015 at a cost of US\$4.25 billion. The world's largest [tunnel boring machine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tunnel_boring_machine), named "[Bertha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bertha_\(tunnel_boring_machine\))", was commissioned for the project, measuring 57 feet (17 m) in diameter. The tunnel's opening was delayed to February 2019 due to issues with the machine, which included a two-year halt in excavation.[^cite_note-385] In 2013, Seattle had the 8th-worst traffic congestion of all American cities, and ranks 10th among all North American cities according to [Inrix](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Inrix).[^cite_note-386]

Seattle has started moving away from the automobile and toward mass transit. From 2004 to 2009, the annual number of unlinked public transportation trips increased by approximately 21%.[^cite_note-387] In 2006, voters in King County passed the Transit Now proposition, which increased bus service hours on high ridership routes and paid for five [limited-stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limited-stop) bus lines called [RapidRide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RapidRide).[^cite_note-388] After rejecting a [roads and transit measure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roads_and_Transit) in 2007, Seattle-area voters passed a transit only measure in 2008 to increase ST Express bus service, extend the [Link light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Link_light_rail) system, and expand and improve [Sounder commuter rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sounder_commuter_rail) service.[^cite_note-389]

A [Link light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Link_light_rail) line (now the [1 Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1_Line_\(Sound_Transit\))) from downtown heading south to Sea-Tac Airport began service in 2009, giving the city its first rapid transit line with intermediate stations within the city limits. The line was first extended north to the [University of Washington](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Washington) in March 2016,[^cite_note-390] followed by [Northgate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northgate,_Seattle) in October 2021,[^cite_note-391] and [Lynnwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lynnwood,_Washington) in August 2024.[^cite_note-392] A second line, the [2 Line](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2_Line_\(Sound_Transit\)) opened in April 2024 between [Bellevue](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bellevue,_Washington) and [Redmond](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Redmond,_Washington) and was extended to Seattle in March 2026. The section between Seattle and Bellevue is the first railway to use a [floating pontoon bridge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pontoon_bridge).[^cite_note-393][^cite_note-Times-Crosslake-394] Voters in the Puget Sound region approved an additional tax increase, part of the [Sound Transit 3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sound_Transit_3) package, in November 2016 to expand light rail to West Seattle and Ballard as well as Tacoma, Everett, and Issaquah.[^cite_note-395]

Seattle has scooter and [bicycle-sharing systems](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle-sharing_system) operated by private companies ([Bird](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bird_Global) and [Lime](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lime_\(transportation_company\))) who cooperate with the city government.[^cite_note-396] A docked bikeshare system, [Pronto Cycle Share](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pronto_Cycle_Share), debuted in 2014 but was shut down in 2017 due to low ridership numbers.[^cite_note-397] Later that year, the city government allowed privately operated [dockless bicycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dockless_bicycle) to operate within Seattle as a pilot.[^cite_note-398] It was later made a permanent program with several competing companies; one used bikes with [gears](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicycle_gearing) specifically chosen for [Seattle's hilly terrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seven_hills_of_Seattle).[^cite_note-auto-89] Later developments included using exclusively [e-bikes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Electric_bicycle) and [e-scooters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E-scooters).[^cite_note-399] A city-operated site tracks ridership.[^cite_note-401] In 2024, a total of 6.3 million trips on bikeshare and scootershare systems were taken in Seattle; there were also 163 serious injuries that year involving bikes and scooters.[^cite_note-402]

### Utilities

Main article: [Utilities of Seattle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Utilities_of_Seattle)

Water and electric power are municipal services, provided by [Seattle Public Utilities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Public_Utilities) and [Seattle City Light](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_City_Light), respectively.[^cite_note-403] Private utility companies serving Seattle include [Puget Sound Energy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puget_Sound_Energy), which has 150,000 [natural gas](22131.md) customers in the city,[^cite_note-404] and [Seattle Steam Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seattle_Steam_Company), which has a [district heating](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/District_heating) system in Downtown Seattle powered by several steam plants.[^cite_note-405] Curbside [solid waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_solid_waste), recycling, and composting pickup is outsourced by Seattle Public Utilities to [Waste Management, Inc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waste_Management,_Inc) and [Recology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Recology). Non-garbage waste is processed by other companies;[^cite_note-406] since 2015, curbside collection of [food waste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Food_waste) for [composting](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Compost) in Seattle has been mandatory to offer to all households.[^cite_note-407]

[Lumen Technologies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lumen_Technologies) (formerly CenturyLink),[^cite_note-408] [Astound Broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Astound_Broadband) (formerly [Wave Broadband](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wave_Broadband)),[^cite_note-409] and [Comcast](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comcast) are the primary providers of [telephone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telephone), [cable television](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cable_television), and [Internet service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internet_service) in the city.[^cite_note-410] CenturyLink stopped providing cable television service in 2021.[^cite_note-411]

As of 2023, at least 88% of Seattle's electricity is produced using [hydropower](14073.md), with less than 40% of the [hydroelectricity](381399.md) acquired via the [Bonneville Power Administration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonneville_Power_Administration). The remaining known electricity sources consist of [wind power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wind_power), [nuclear power](22153.md), and [biogas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biogas); less than 2% comes from an unidentified source.[^cite_note-412] Seattle Public Utilities manages two [tap water](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tap_water) supply systems on the [Cedar River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cedar_River_\(Washington\)) and [Tolt River](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tolt_River).[^cite_note-413] These systems are fed by melted [snowpack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Snowpack) in the Cascade Mountains over the autumn and winter that fill reservoirs as they melt.[^cite_note-414] Seattle's [wastewater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wastewater) system includes 1,422 miles (2,288 km) of sewers that reach [treatment plants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wastewater_treatment) that discharge into Puget Sound; a 485-mile (781 km) network of separate tunnels for [stormwater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stormwater) serve other treatment facilities.[^cite_note-415] Older areas of the city have a [combined sewer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Combined_sewer) system that dumps stormwater and untreated wastewater into Puget Sound during overflow events.[^cite_note-416]

## Crime



A 2023 map of recorded crimes in each Seattle neighborhood

In 2023, Seattle had 5,000 violent [crimes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime). From 2013 to 2018 there was a slow increase in crimes. The crime rate dipped in 2020, before increasing in 2021 and 2022. In 2023, Seattle had a violent crime rate of 683 per 100,000 people, and 5,174 property crimes per 100,000 people.[^cite_note-417]

Overall crimes per capita in Seattle decreased slightly during the beginning of the 21st century. While the total number of crimes rose, the population of the city[^cite_note-OPCDCensus-418] grew faster. For the data below, per-capita estimates yield the following: 6,744 crimes per 100,000 people for 2008–2009, 6,725 crimes per 100,000 people for 2010–2019, and 6,325 crimes per 100,000 people for 2020–2024.[^cite_note-419] These percentages are overestimates due to the sample rate of the census: city crime statistics are updated yearly, but city population figures are only updated once per decade.

| Year | Violent crimes | Property crimes | Total | Pop. est.[^cite_note-OPCDCensus-418] |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 2008 | 3,301 | 33,542 | 36,843 | 563,374 |
| 2009 | 3,672 | 35,476 | 39,148 |  |
| 2010 | 3,293 | 33,736 | 37,029 | 608,660 |
| 2011 | 3,357 | 32,503 | 35,860 |  |
| 2012 | 3,580 | 32,598 | 36,178 |  |
| 2013 | 3,564 | 37,168 | 40,732 |  |
| 2014 | 3,783 | 41,029 | 44,812 |  |
| 2015 | 3,831 | 38,191 | 42,022 |  |
| 2016 | 4,060 | 38,999 | 43,059 |  |

[^cite_note-1]: &#x20;November 13, 1851, is often referred to as the unofficial date of Seattle's founding, when much of the [Denny Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Denny_Party) arrived at [Alki Point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alki_Point). However, the first White settlers to inhabit the area had already arrived in September, which included some scouts of the Denny clan. The modern city did not take shape until the following spring after much of the party abandoned Alki to move across [the bay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Elliott_Bay). The name "Seattle" didn't become official until May 23, 1853.
